## Q1-r1

arm `folder`, model `claude-haiku-5-5`, tools `Read,Grep,Glob`, effort `low`, wall 24.7 s

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Frequency: across all five years, what percentage of cases have base section 16(h), and what percentage have base section 20(a)(2)? Round to one decimal.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Answer:** 39.6% of cases have base section 16(h) (3,941 of 9,958). 22.9% have base section 20(a)(2) (2,284 of 9,958).

**Reading taken:** A case is one row (one form page), so the denominator is all 9,958 rows. Base section is the cite with "IC 8-1-26-" and any parenthesized word removed. Because each row has one cite, the count of matching rows is the count of cases.

**Computation:** The Bash tool was disabled in this session, so I could not run `cases.py`. I counted rows with the Grep tool on `reference/cases.csv` instead. The base-section tests were:
- 16(h): `IC 8-1-26-16\(h\)` matched 3,941 lines. 3,941 / 9,958 = 39.58%, which rounds to 39.6%.
- 20(a)(2): `IC 8-1-26-20\(a\)\(2\)` matched 2,284 lines. 2,284 / 9,958 = 22.94%, which rounds to 22.9%.

The file has 9,959 lines, which is the header plus 9,958 rows. The prefix `IC 8-1-26-` appears in 9,957 rows, so one row has a blank cite, as the schema says. No OCR-misread cites (`16(b)`, `20(a)(l)`, `IC 8-l`) remain in the file. Those percentages do not change if the blank-cite row is dropped from the denominator (9,957 gives 39.6% and 22.9%).

**Receipts:**
- 16(h): ord_PSD_011525.pdf p25 case 32960; ord_PSD_011525.pdf p26 case 32698; ord_PSD_011525.pdf p27 case 32683
- 20(a)(2): ord_PSD_011525.pdf p19 case 32551; ord_PSD_011525.pdf p20 case 32579; ord_PSD_011525.pdf p21 case 32499

**Caveat:** These are exact string counts of the cite text, not the tool's parsed base-section output. The tool was not run, so the `--base` filter results are not confirmed.
```

  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".

**Answer:** Base section 16(h) is **39.6%** of cases (3,941 of 9,958). Base section 20(a)(2) is **22.9%** (2,284 of 9,958).

**Reading taken:** A case is one row (one form page), so the denominator is all 9,958 rows in `reference/cases.csv`, across order years 2021–2025. Base section is the cite with the prefix and any parenthesized word removed, so "16(h)" with or without a qualifier counts as 16(h). Rows with a blank cite or case number 0 stay in the denominator. Excluding them would give 39.58% and 22.94%, which round to the same figures.

**Computation:** I could not run `cases.py`, because the Bash tool is disabled in this session. I counted lines with Grep instead. The file has 9,959 lines: a header plus 9,958 rows.
- `IC 8-1-26-16\(h\)`: 3,941 lines. 3,941 / 9,958 = 39.58% → 39.6%.
- `IC 8-1-26-20\(a\)\(2\)`: 2,284 lines. 2,284 / 9,958 = 22.94% → 22.9%.

**Checks:**
- All 9,957 non-blank cites match one of the nine allowed sections, and no OCR-misread strings (such as `8-l-26`, `(l)`, `lS(` or `16(b)`) remain in this file. So the counts should match the corrected data, but I did not verify this against `corrections.csv`, which is not in the folder.
- Receipts (order file, page, case number) are not included. They need the tool, or a line-by-line read of the CSV.
```

You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Trend: how many cases are there in each year 2021, 2022, 2023, 2024 and 2025, and which year has the most?

  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".

  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).

  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Answer**

- **Utility-respondent cases:** 0.0% have a "warning letter" recommendation (1 of 2,715).
- **Non-utility-respondent cases:** 28.1% have one (2,032 of 7,243).

**How I got these**

- The Bash tool was disabled in this session, so I counted with Grep on `folder/reference/cases.csv` (the raw parse, 9,958 rows, all dated 2021–2025). I did not have `cases_clean.csv` or `corrections.csv`, so the raw recommendation text is used.
- Utility respondent is decided by the `respondent` column only, using the preamble prefixes. That gives 2,715 utility rows and 7,243 non-utility rows, which sum to 9,958.
- "Warning letter" in the recommendation: 2,033 rows match case-insensitively. Two of the 2,035 rows containing the phrase have it only in the `additional_info` note (rows 563 and 8779), so they are excluded.
- The one utility-respondent match is Citizens Energy Group - Steam Division (ord_PSD_113022.pdf, page 175).
- Non-utility warnings = 2,033 − 1 = 2,032. 2,032 / 7,243 = 28.05%, which rounds to 28.1%.

**Caveat:** The raw parse still has the known recommendation defects the schema lists. The checkbox-form pages and the `$$1000` rows contain no warning-letter wording, so they should not change these counts. The duplicate case-number rows are counted as separate pages, as the definition of a case requires.
```

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Warnings: what percentage of utility-respondent cases, and what percentage of non-utility-respondent cases, have a recommendation containing the words "warning letter" (any capitalization)? Round to one decimal.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Warning letter shares, by respondent type (order dates 2021–2025):**

- **Utility-respondent cases: 0.0%** (1 of 2,715 cases)
- **Non-utility-respondent cases: 28.1%** (2,032 of 7,243 cases)

**Basis:** I counted from `reference/cases.csv`, the raw parse, which has 9,958 case rows from 58 orders. Bash was disabled in this session, so I used regex counts with Grep on the CSV.

- Utility respondents are the rows whose respondent starts with one of the 13 listed prefixes, ignoring case and leading spaces. That gives 2,715 rows. The only one whose recommendation contains "warning letter" is ord_PSD_113022.pdf page 175 (respondent "Citizens Energy Group - Steam Division").
- "Warning letter" is counted only in the recommendation field. Two rows mention it only in `additional_info` (ord_PSD_012622.pdf p.102 and ord_PSD_113022.pdf p.211), so they are excluded. The recommendation-field count is 2,033 rows, and 1 of those is utility, leaving 2,032 non-utility rows.
- Non-utility cases are the remaining 9,958 − 2,715 = 7,243 rows.

**Sensitivity:** `cases_clean.csv` and `corrections.csv` are not in the folder. None of the ledger corrections touch a recommendation that contains "warning letter". The one `0` case row is non-utility and has no warning letter. Dropping it gives 2,032 / 7,242 = 28.06%, which still rounds to 28.1%.
```

## Q4-r3

arm `folder`, model `claude-haiku-5-5`, tools `Read,Grep,Glob`, effort `low`, wall 71.5 s

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Warnings: what percentage of utility-respondent cases, and what percentage of non-utility-respondent cases, have a recommendation containing the words "warning letter" (any capitalization)? Round to one decimal.
```

- **Utility-respondent cases with "warning letter": 0.0%** (1 of 2,715)
- **Non-utility-respondent cases with "warning letter": 28.1%** (2,032 of 7,243)

**How I got them**

Bash was disabled in this session, so I couldn't run the helper script `reference/tools/cases.py`. I counted with Grep on `reference/cases.csv` instead.

- The file has 9,958 case rows (9,959 lines including the header). That matches the schema.
- Utility respondents: 2,715 rows whose respondent starts with one of the 13 listed prefixes, ignoring case and leading spaces. Quoted respondents with commas are included.
- Non-utility respondents: 9,958 − 2,715 = 7,243.
- "Warning letter" (any case) in the recommendation: 2,033 rows overall. Only one of these is a utility respondent: Citizens Energy Group – Steam Division, order ord_PSD_113022.pdf, page 175, case 27398. That gives 2,032 non-utility rows.

**Caveats**

- A plain search for "warning letter" anywhere in the file finds 2,035 lines, two more than the recommendation-field match. I could not identify those two lines. Even if both were non-utility, the rate would still round to 28.1%.
- The row with case number `0` (ord_PSD_020321.pdf page 27) is counted as a non-utility case. Its recommendation is not a warning letter, so it does not change either figure.
- Rows are counted as cases as the definitions specify, so the 23 case numbers that repeat across orders are counted once per row.
```

Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Training option: among cases with a headline penalty above $0, what percentage have a recommendation that contains the word "training" (any capitalization)? Round to one decimal.

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Answer: 44.7%** (3,530 of 7,896 cases with a headline penalty above $0 have "training" in the recommendation).

**Reading taken:** "Training" matched case-insensitively as a substring of the recommendation field. Headline penalty above $0 means the recommendation contains a "$" amount whose first value is non-zero.

**Method:** The helper script could not run because Bash is disabled in this session. I counted with Grep against `folder/reference/cases.csv`, anchoring on the `IC 8-1-26-` cite column so the recommendation field is isolated. This is not the tool's own output, so please rerun `python3 reference/tools/cases.py count --expr "r['headline']>0 and 'training' in r['recommendation'].lower()"` (numerator) and `count --expr "r['headline']>0"` (denominator) to confirm.

- Rows with a cite pattern matched: 9,956 of 9,958 (the 2 unmatched rows are likely the blank-cite or quoted-cite rows).
- Recommendations with no "$" at all: 2,060. None of these contain "training", so every training case has a "$" amount.
- Recommendations with "$" and a first amount of $0: none found.
- Denominator (headline > $0): 9,956 − 2,060 = 7,896.
- Numerator (headline > $0 and "training"): 3,530.
- 3,530 / 7,896 = 44.70%.

**Caveats:**
- The 2 unmatched rows could shift the result. If both were training cases with headline above $0, the result would be 44.72%, still 44.7%.
- Headline penalty uses the first "$" figure as printed. "Training or $5,000 if…" therefore counts with headline $5,000, as the definition requires.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Answer: 44.7%** (3,531 of 7,905 cases with a headline penalty above $0 have "training" in the recommendation).

I could not run the helper script because Bash is disabled in this session. I got the counts with Grep on `reference/cases.csv` instead, anchoring each pattern to the recommendation column (field 11) so that training mentioned in other columns is not counted.

- **Rows:** all 9,958 rows are dated 2021–2025, so no year filter removes any.
- **Training in the recommendation:** 3,549 rows. Of these, 18 have no dollar sign (for example `Training (completed)`), so 3,531 have a dollar amount.
- **Headline penalty above $0:** 7,898 rows have a dollar amount followed by 1–9. I added the 7 `$$1000` rows, which the pattern misses, for 7,905. The only zero-amount row with a dollar sign is the `$0 penalty` note, and that sits in the additional-information column, not the recommendation.
- **Result:** 3,531 / 7,905 = 44.67%, which rounds to 44.7%.

Caveats:
- I used the raw `cases.csv`, not `cases_clean.csv`, which is not in the folder. The 8 checkbox-form rows in the raw file list every option, but every option on those forms mentions training and carries a dollar amount, so they count the same way before and after correction.
- The 7,905 denominator comes from pattern counts, not a row-by-row check, so treat the last digit as approximate. The rounded figure is 44.7%.
```

You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Training option: among cases with a headline penalty above $0, what percentage have a recommendation that contains the word "training" (any capitalization)? Round to one decimal.

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Estimate: about 44.8%, not computed exactly.** I could not get the exact figure. The Bash tool is disabled in this session, so I could not run `reference/tools/cases.py`. I also cannot load the 2 MB CSV whole.

**What I could do (Grep counts on `reference/cases.csv`, which has 9,958 rows plus a header):**

- Lines containing "training" (any case): 3,556
- Lines with a non-zero dollar amount anywhere (`\$+\s*[1-9]`): 7,900. This is a proxy for headline penalty above $0.
- Lines with both "training" and a non-zero dollar amount: 3,537

The estimate is 3,537 / 7,900 ≈ 44.8%.

**Why it is an estimate:**

- The proxy is not exact. A line can have a non-zero dollar amount only after a leading "$0", or only in the `additional_info` column. Such rows have headline $0 by the definition but are counted here. A regex check found about 8 rows with a leading zero-dollar pattern, so the error is small.
- Both figures are line counts, so a quoted recommendation spanning lines would be miscounted. I found no evidence of that.
- Exact figures need `python3 reference/tools/cases.py count --penalty-min 0.0000001 --rec-contains training`, divided by `count --penalty-min 0.0000001`. Rounded to one decimal, that gives the answer exactly.
```

  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".

  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).

  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",

**Final answer** (verbatim):

````
I could not compute the repeat-concentration figures. The Bash tool is disabled in this session, so I could not run a script over the data. I also did not tally the names by hand: `reference/cases.csv` has 9,958 rows, and a manual count of that many normalized names would not be reliable. So I have no count of respondents with 5 or more non-utility cases and no percentage.

What I did establish:

- **Data available:** `folder/reference/cases.csv` (raw parse, 14 columns, one row per recommendation form page). `SCHEMA.md` says `cases_clean.csv` has the corrections applied, but that file is not in the folder, so I would use the raw file.
- **Filter:** keep rows with `order_date` from 2021-01-01 to 2025-12-31. The schema gives the order dates as 2021-02-03 to 2025-12-10, which fits the 58 orders in scope.
- **Duplicates:** 4 rows are duplicate `(order_file, case_number)` pairs (case 25064 on ord_PSD_051822 pages 7 and 346 is two real forms; case 33669 on ord_PSD_101525 pages 245 and 246 is one repeated page). Per your definition, a case is one row on one page, so I would keep all rows, but note the 33669 pair.
- **Known defect:** one row has case number `0` (ord_PSD_020321.pdf page 27) and a garbled respondent (`130 2nd St NE`). It is a non-utility row and would count toward the total unless you exclude it.

The script to run once Bash or Python is available (save outside the data folder, run with `python3 -I`):

```python
import csv, re, sys
from collections import Counter
PREF = ["CenterPoint","Northern Indiana Public Service","NIPSCO","Citizens Gas","Citizens Energy",
        "Vectren","Indiana American Water","Ohio Valley Gas","American Electric Power","Duke Energy",
        "Community Natural Gas","Midwest Natural Gas","Frontier Communications"]
SUF = {"inc","llc","co","corp","corporation","company"}
def is_util(r):
    s = r.lstrip().casefold()
    return any(s.startswith(p.casefold()) for p in PREF)
def norm(name):
    s = "".join(c for c in name.lower() if c.isalpha() or c.isdigit() or c == " ")
    parts = re.sub(" +", " ", s).strip().split(" ")
    if parts and parts[-1] in SUF: parts = parts[:-1]
    return " ".join(parts)
rows = [r for r in csv.DictReader(open(sys.argv[1], newline="", encoding="utf-8"))
        if "2021-01-01" <= r["order_date"] <= "2025-12-31"]
nu = [r for r in rows if not is_util(r["respondent"])]
c = Counter(norm(r["respondent"]) for r in nu)
big = {k: v for k, v in c.items() if v >= 5}
print("non-utility cases:", len(nu), "| distinct:", len(c), "| >=5:", len(big))
print("cases covered:", sum(big.values()), "| share %:", round(100*sum(big.values())/len(nu), 1))
```

Run it as `python3 -I script.py /tmp/r2run-6z68tann/folder/reference/cases.csv`. Note that the script does not use the `_`-stripping of `norm_name` in `cases.py`, which is a different normalization from the one you specified; the script above follows your steps.

**Answer: not computed.** The number of respondents with 5+ cases, and the percentage of non-utility cases they cover, need the script run on the data.
````

What I could confirm from the files:
- `reference/cases.csv` has 1,599 rows whose order date is in 2021 and 1,684 in 2025 (by Grep on the `order_date` column). These are all rows in those years, before the non-utility filter.
- Working out the mean by hand means applying the first-dollar-amount rule to each recommendation and the utility-prefix test to each respondent. Across about 3,300 rows that is too error-prone to do without running code.

To get the answer, run these from the folder root (the mean is the `mean=` value in the output, and the `n=` line is the denominator, which includes $0 cases):

```
python3 reference/tools/cases.py stat --field headline --year 2021 --utility no
python3 reference/tools/cases.py stat --field headline --year 2025 --utility no
python3 reference/tools/cases.py group --by year --stat headline --utility no --sort key
```

The `stat` output includes a line counting zero-value rows, so you can check the $0 cases are in the denominator. Both commands should be run before the result is quoted, since the headline penalty is sensitive to the `$$1000` and `$3, 500` rows the SCHEMA notes.
````

**Final answer** (verbatim):

I could not compute these two numbers. The Bash tool is disabled in this session, and the only way to run the helper script (`reference/tools/cases.py`) is through Bash. Reading the raw CSV and summing penalties by hand is not reliable at this size: there are 1,599 rows with a 2021 order date and 1,684 with a 2025 order date, before filtering out utility respondents. I did not want to give a mean I had not checked, so I have no value to report for either year.

**Commands to run (from the `folder` directory):**

```
python3 -I reference/tools/cases.py --csv reference/cases.csv stat --field headline --year 2021 --utility no
python3 -I reference/tools/cases.py --csv reference/cases.csv stat --field headline --year 2025 --utility no
```

The `stat` output reports `mean` and `n`, and includes $0 rows by default, which matches the question. Round the mean to the nearest dollar.

**Data issues to check before you trust the output:**

- **Use the corrected file if it exists.** `SCHEMA.md` says the 8 checkbox-form recommendations in `ord_PSD_042121.pdf` (pages 149–156, dated 2021) are wrong in `cases.csv`. The raw text holds every option, so the parser reads `$5,000` from "Training or $5,000 civil penalty…", which is not the ticked option. The corrected values are in `cases_clean.csv`, but that file is not in the folder. Without it, the 2021 mean is off for those rows.
- **Dollar-sign oddities.** Seven rows read `$$1000` (the tool handles this) and one reads `$3, 500` (the tool reads it as $3, not $3,500, because the parser stops at the space). `ord_PSD_122821.pdf` page 4 is the one affected, and it is a 2021 order only if its date falls in 2021; the file name suggests 2021-12-28 (MMDDYY `122821`), so it counts toward the 2021 mean.
- **Missing dollar signs.** `3000` (ord_PSD_060822.pdf page 290) and `0` (ord_PSD_080322.pdf page 222) have no `$`. The tool would give the first as 0 and the second as 0. Check whether these matter for the year you need.
- **Page 27 of `ord_PSD_020321.pdf`** has case number `0` and is unusable for case-level counts. It is a 2021 order, and the tool includes it unless you exclude it with `--exclude-bad`. Run the 2021 command with `--exclude-bad` to drop it.

If you run the commands and paste the `n` and `mean` lines back, I can check them against the data notes above.
````

  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".

**Answer: 446 cases have a recommendation containing "ticket escalation" (any capitalization). The most common headline penalty among them is $1,500, in 291 of those cases.**

How I got these from `folder/reference/cases.csv`:

- **Count (446):** I searched case-insensitively for lines starting with `ord_` that contain "ticket escalation". This gave 446, and the unanchored search gave the same figure. Each row sits on one line, so this is one match per case.
- **Mode ($1,500, 291):** I counted rows whose recommendation begins with each leading amount. The $1,500 group (291) is more than half of the 446 and far ahead of the next group ($3,000, 56 rows; $5,000, 46 rows). So the mode does not depend on the remaining rows.

Caveats:

- I had no shell, so the counts come from regex searches over the raw CSV, not from the `cases.py` helper. The 32 rows not in the breakdown start with other amounts such as $3,500 and $4,000; I did not tally them individually, but none could overtake $1,500.
- The data folder's schema describes a corrected `cases_clean.csv`, which is not in the folder. The raw file is the only source I used. The known defects (`$$1000`, `$3, 500`) do not affect the $1,500 result.
- The raw file's 9,958 rows include the duplicate-page rows the schema describes. The count is of rows, not distinct case numbers.
```

**Answer: 446 cases contain "ticket escalation", and the most common headline penalty among them is $1,500 (291 of the 446 cases, 65.2%).**

I could not run the helper script because Bash is disabled in this session. I counted from `reference/cases.csv` with Grep instead, so these figures are not from `cases.py`.

- **Count:** I matched "ticket escalation" case-insensitively, anchored to the recommendation field (the column right after the cite). That gives 446 rows, one per case, with no duplicate-case adjustment (the known duplicates are unlikely to affect this count).
- **Headline penalties:** the first dollar amount for these 446 breaks down as:
  - $1,500: 291 (e.g. "$1,500, respondent can complete training instead of paying $1,500; no ticket escalation - additional $1,000")
  - $3,000: 56
  - $3,500: 22
  - $4,000: 15
  - $5,000: 46
  - $1,000 or other: the remaining 16

The $1,500 headline is well ahead of the next most common amount.

The literal count includes recommendations that say "no ticket escalation", since they contain the phrase. If you meant only recommendations where escalation was applied, the count and the mode would both change; I did not compute that version.
```

You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Escalation: how many cases have a recommendation containing "ticket escalation" (any capitalization), and what is the most common headline penalty among those cases?
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**446 cases** have a recommendation containing "ticket escalation" (any capitalization). The most common headline penalty among them is **$1,500**, with **291 of 446 cases (65.2%)**.

**Method:** Bash is disabled in this session, so I could not run `cases.py`. I used Grep on `reference/cases.csv` instead, which is less rigorous than the tool. The file has 9,959 lines (one header plus 9,958 case rows), so each line is one case. Every match sits in the `recommendation` column.

- Count of lines containing "ticket escalation" (case-insensitive): **446**.
- Lines where the recommendation begins with "$1,500" and contains "ticket escalation": **291** (both the `$1,500,` and `$1,500;` forms, and the pattern with no separator, return 291).
- Lines beginning with "$5,000" and containing the phrase: 46. Lines beginning with "$1,000" or "$2,500": 0.
- The remaining 109 cases are not broken down. None of them can reach 291, so $1,500 is the mode regardless.

**Caveats:**
- The phrase usually appears as a negation, e.g. "no ticket escalation - additional $1,000". I counted it literally, as the question asks.
- The headline is the first dollar amount as defined. For the 291 cases that is $1,500, which is the training alternative ("$1,500, respondent can complete training instead of paying $1,500"), not a fine.
- Receipts (order_file, page, case_number): ord_PSD_011525.pdf, page 102, case 32723; ord_PSD_011525.pdf, page 101, case 32841; ord_PSD_011525.pdf, page 119, case 32587.
```

  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".

The two can give different top names if spelling variants are present.

To get the answer, run this from the folder root:

```
python3 -I reference/tools/cases.py group --by other_party --utility no --sort count --top 5
```

Then divide the top count by the kept-row total that the command prints, and round to one decimal. Note that the helper's grouping is slightly looser than the exact-text rule above. ## Q10-r2

arm `folder`, model `claude-haiku-5-5`, tools `Read,Grep,Glob`, effort `low`, wall 29.6 s

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Expired tickets: among cases with base section 16(h), what percentage have the qualifier "expire", and what percentage have the qualifier "prior"? Round to one decimal.
```

The figures are not corrected for OCR errors. `cases_clean.csv` and `corrections.csv` are not in the folder. `SCHEMA.md` says 16 cites were misread by OCR, including one `16(b)` that the page image shows as `16(h)`. Some of those rows are probably 16(h) cases that the raw file does not count, so the true denominator is likely a little higher. I cannot quantify the effect without the corrected file. Based on 16 rows out of about 9,958, the shift should be well under one percentage point.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Expired tickets, base section 16(h):**

- **"expire" qualifier: 12.3%** (483 of 3,941 cases)
- **"prior" qualifier: 6.0%** (236 of 3,941 cases)

Method: I counted rows in `reference/cases.csv` (the raw parse) whose cite is `IC 8-1-26-16(h)`, with or without a qualifier, and then counted the rows with `(expire)` and `(prior)`. Bash was disabled in this session, so I used Grep line counts rather than the `cases.py` helper. Each row has exactly one cite, so line counts equal case counts.

Caveat: `SCHEMA.md` describes a corrected file, `cases_clean.csv`, in which 16 OCR-misread cites are fixed. Some of those fixes would move rows into or out of base 16(h), and that file and its corrections ledger are not in the folder. So these figures are for the raw parse. The denominator could shift by roughly a dozen rows, which would move the percentages by a few tenths of a point at most.
```

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Geography: define a case's **city** as the text after the last comma of its damage location, trimmed of spaces and lowercased, with spelling variants not merged; cases whose damage location has no comma or an empty city are left out of this question. Which city has the most cases, how many cases is that, and what percentage of the cases that have a city do the five most common cities together account for? Round the percentage to one decimal.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Answer (partly verified):**

- **Most common city: Indianapolis, 1,153 cases.**
- **Share of the five most common cities: 26.2%.** The top five are Indianapolis 1,153, Fort Wayne 529, Evansville 445, South Bend 295 and Bloomington 184, which total 2,606 of about 9,943 cases with a city.

**How I got these:**
- I used the raw `folder/reference/cases.csv` (one row per case, as the SCHEMA defines a case). I counted rows whose quoted damage location has a comma and a non-empty city, followed by an IC 8-1-26 cite. Matching is case-insensitive, which matches the lowercasing rule. Spelling variants are not merged.
- Bash was disabled in this session, so I could not run a script. Every count came from Grep pattern counts, not from a full tally.
- The denominator of about 9,943 is the count of rows with a non-empty city. It may be off by one or two rows (for example, a row with a blank violation field).

**Caveats:**
- Bloomington (184) is the fifth city, but Elkhart (179) and Greenwood (169) are close behind. About 3,400 rows fall in cities I did not count individually. If one of those cities has more than 184 cases, the fifth place and the 26.2% would change. I could not rule that out with Grep alone. Treat the 26.2% as an estimate. The Indianapolis count of 1,153 is solid.
- Fort Wayne also has variant spellings ("Ft Wayne", "Ft. Wayne"). These are not merged, as the definition requires, and they affect only the tail, not the top five.
```

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Geography: define a case's **city** as the text after the last comma of its damage location, trimmed of spaces and lowercased, with spelling variants not merged; cases whose damage location has no comma or an empty city are left out of this question. Which city has the most cases, how many cases is that, and what percentage of the cases that have a city do the five most common cities together account for? Round the percentage to one decimal.
```

**Answer (partly estimated): Indianapolis is the top city with 1,153 cases. The top-five share is an estimate of about 26.2%, not a verified figure.**

I could not finish this exactly. The Bash tool was disabled, so I could not run a script over `reference/cases.csv`. I counted cities with case-insensitive regex searches instead, and that method cannot produce a complete city tally.

**What I could verify (counts of rows whose city, after the last comma, is the name shown):**

| City | Cases |
|---|---|
| indianapolis | 1,153 |
| fort wayne | 530 |
| evansville | 445 |
| south bend | 295 |
| elkhart | 179 |
| greenwood | 169 (checked as part of a group, not individually) |
| carmel | 159 |

Other checked cities, all below 179: terre haute 154, gary 156, valparaiso 144, lafayette 111, anderson 114, muncie 105, columbus 108, fishers 88, hammond 43.

**Why it is an estimate:**
- The checked cities account for about 4,200 of roughly 9,946 rows with a city. About 5,700 rows sit in cities I did not enumerate. A city I never checked could exceed 179 and displace Elkhart from fifth place.
- The denominator is about 9,946: the 9,958 rows minus about 12 with no comma or a blank location (9 blank, 3 without a city). I did not confirm this exactly.

**Estimate:** the top five (Indianapolis, Fort Wayne, Evansville, South Bend, Elkhart) total about 2,602 cases. That is 2,602 / 9,946 ≈ **26.2%**. Treat this as an estimate. The top-city count of 1,153 is exact for the bare name, though a few variant spellings could add to it.

To get the exact answer, run `python3 reference/tools/cases.py group --by city --top 5` from the folder root, or rerun this with Bash enabled.
```

  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".

  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",

I could not compute these numbers. The Bash tool is disabled in this session, and I have no other way to run Python over `reference/cases.csv`, which has 9,958 rows. I read the tool code and the data dictionary, but I did not read the rows, so I have no figures to report.

**Results**

- Mean lag, 2023 orders: not computed.
- Mean lag, 2024 orders: not computed.
- Share of cases with lag over 365 days: not computed.

**How to get them**

The tool already computes `lag_days` (order date minus damage date, in days) and `year` (order year). From the folder root, this prints the three numbers:

```
python3 -I -c "import sys; sys.path.insert(0,'reference/tools'); import cases; rows=cases.load('reference/cases.csv'); ok=[r for r in rows if r['lag_days']!='']; print('parseable n =',len(ok)); [print(y,'n=',len([r for r in ok if r['year']==y]),'mean=',round(sum(r['lag_days'] for r in ok if r['year']==y)/len([r for r in ok if r['year']==y]),2)) for y in (2023,2024)]; o=[r for r in ok if r['lag_days']>365]; print('>365:',len(o),'of',len(ok),'=',round(100*len(o)/len(ok),1),'%')"
```

Two points to settle when you run it:

- **Denominator:** the command counts every row with a parseable damage date. Row 0 (`ord_PSD_020321.pdf` page 27) has a broken case number and a non-date advisory meeting. Its damage date may still parse, so it could be in the count. Dropping it changes the denominator by one.
- **Rounding:** the question asks for whole days for the means, so round the printed means to the nearest integer.
````

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Reporting lag: for each case with a parseable damage date (month/day/year), define the **lag** as the number of calendar days from the damage date to the order date. Using the order's year (not the damage year), what is the mean lag in days for cases in 2023 and in 2024, each rounded to the nearest whole day, and what percentage of all such cases (all years) have a lag of more than 365 days? Round the percentage to one decimal.
```

```

- **Case `0` row:** `ord_PSD_020321.pdf` page 27 has an unusable case number. Its damage date may still parse, so it could be included in the lag figures. Add `--exclude-bad` to drop it, and compare the results with and without it.

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Results (order year, share of cases with a recorded abstention):**

- **2021: 41.4%** (662 of 1,599 cases)
- **2024: 54.0%** (1,025 of 1,897 cases)
- **2025: 49.8%** (838 of 1,684 cases)

Denominators are every row in `reference/cases.csv` with that order year, so the row with case number `0` and the duplicate-page rows are included. Without a shell, I counted with regex searches over the CSV rather than running the helper script, so these are hand-verified counts, not script output.

**How the counts were made:**
- Blank abstentions were found from the line ending (`…,abstentions,additional_info,yes|no`). Rows with a comma-containing quoted note were counted separately: 7 in 2021, 1 in 2024, 1 in 2025.
- Row 8038 (2021, ord_PSD_110321.pdf) has abstentions that is only the stray text "Additional Infonnation:", so I counted it as blank.
- Row 8005 (2021) reads "Smith Additional Infonnation:", which I counted as a recorded abstention ("Smith").
- The only stray-text rows in 2024 and 2025 are absent from the data, so no other adjustments were needed.
```

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Abstentions: a case has a **recorded abstention** if its Abstentions field has any text left after trimming spaces and deleting any stray words "Additional Infonnation:" or "Additional Information:" that leaked into it. What percentage of cases in each of 2021, 2024 and 2025 (by order year) have a recorded abstention? Round each to one decimal.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Answers (by order year, share of cases with a recorded abstention):**

- **2021: 662 of 1,599 (41.4%)**
- **2024: 1,025 of 1,897 (54.0%)**
- **2025: 838 of 1,684 (49.8%)**

I could not run the tool's Python here because Bash is disabled in this session. I counted with Grep regex on `reference/cases.csv` instead, so these are not `cases.py` outputs. The counts reconcile: 2021 + 2024 + 2025 = 5,180 rows, and across all years 4,923 rows have a recorded abstention and 5,035 are blank, which matches the 5,035 blank count in SCHEMA.md.

**Reading taken:** a case is one row, and "recorded abstention" means the Abstentions field is non-blank after trimming spaces and removing the stray "Additional Infonnation:" / "Additional Information:" text.

**Stray-text handling:**
- ord_PSD_110321.pdf page 20 (2021): the field reads "Smith Additional Infonnation:", which cleans to "Smith", so it counts as recorded.
- ord_PSD_110321.pdf page 53 (2021): the field reads only "Additional Infonnation:", which cleans to blank, so it does not count. I subtracted it from the raw count (663 → 662).

**Receipts (2021 recorded abstentions, first few rows):** I did not select receipts by a tool run, so these are examples from the counted rows, not the full set.
- ord_PSD_110321.pdf, page 20, case 23285, abstention "Smith Additional Infonnation:" (cleans to "Smith")
- ord_PSD_030321.pdf, page 29, case 22008 (abstention blank; counted as not recorded, shown only because it has the escaped-quote recommendation row I handled)

**Caveats:**
- Counts use a regex anchored on the cite field. I checked that every 2021, 2024 and 2025 row matches either the recorded or the blank pattern, with one exception: a 2021 row (ord_PSD_030321.pdf, page 29) has embedded escaped quotes in its recommendation and is blank, which I confirmed by reading the line.
- The percentages are rounded to one decimal place from 662/1,599, 1,025/1,897 and 838/1,684.
```

You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Abstentions: a case has a **recorded abstention** if its Abstentions field has any text left after trimming spaces and deleting any stray words "Additional Infonnation:" or "Additional Information:" that leaked into it. What percentage of cases in each of 2021, 2024 and 2025 (by order year) have a recorded abstention? Round each to one decimal.

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Answer (by order year, raw parse `folder/reference/cases.csv`):**

- **2021: 41.5%**. 663 of 1,599 cases have a recorded abstention.
- **2024: 54.0%**. 1,025 of 1,897 cases.
- **2025: 49.8%**. 838 of 1,684 cases.

**Reading taken:** a case has a recorded abstention if the Abstentions field has text left after trimming and removing any "Additional Information:" leakage. Case = one row, as the preamble defines it.

**How I got the numbers:** Bash was disabled in this session, so I could not run `cases.py` or a Python script. I counted with Grep regex on the CSV lines instead:
- Rows per order year: 2021 = 1,599; 2024 = 1,897; 2025 = 1,684.
- Rows with a blank Abstentions field (the field sits just before Additional Information and the scan flag): 2021 = 935; 2024 = 872; 2025 = 846.
- Recorded = total − blank: 2021 = 664; 2024 = 1,025; 2025 = 838.
- Leakage: two 2021 rows carry "Additional Infonnation:" in the Abstentions field. Row 8038 (ord_PSD_110321.pdf, page 53) holds only that text, so after cleaning it is blank and I subtracted it, giving 2021 = 663. Row 8005 (page 20) reads "Smith Additional Infonnation:", which cleans to "Smith" and stays recorded. No 2024 or 2025 rows have this leakage.
- Whitespace-only Abstentions fields: none found.

**Receipts:**
- Blank-abstention example: ord_PSD_110321.pdf page 20 is recorded ("Smith"), but page 53 is not (only the leaked label).
- The 2021 denominator includes ord_PSD_122821.pdf page 82 (blank Abstentions).

**Caveats:**
- `cases_clean.csv` is not in the folder, so I used the raw parse. The corrections ledger does not touch the Abstentions field, so the raw and clean counts should match, but I could not confirm that.
- The per-year counts come from regex on the CSV, not from `cases.py`. A quoted field containing a comma in an unusual position could be misclassified. I found no such case in the checks I ran.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Answers (raw `cases.csv`, all 58 orders are dated 2021–2025, so no year filter is needed):**

- **expire:** 185 of 518 cases have a $0 headline penalty, **35.7%**
- **prior:** 59 of 272 cases have a $0 headline penalty, **21.7%**
- **out:** 35 of 191 cases have a $0 headline penalty, **18.3%**

**How I got these:** Bash is disabled in this session, so I could not run `reference/tools/cases.py`. I counted with Grep on `reference/cases.csv` instead.

1. Qualifier totals: `\(expire\)` 518, `\(prior\)` 272, `\(out\)` 191. These match the counts in SCHEMA.md.
2. Rows with a $ amount: I counted matches of `\(qualifier\)[^\n]*\$`, giving expire 333, prior 213, out 156.
3. Rows with no $ anywhere after the cite (headline $0): I counted matches of `\(qualifier\)[^$\n]*$`, giving expire 185, prior 59, out 35.
4. Check: 333+185=518, 213+59=272, 156+35=191. Every qualifier row falls in one group.

**Caveats:**
- The file is the raw parse. SCHEMA.md lists a corrections ledger (8 checkbox-form recommendations, 16 OCR-garbled cites) that is not in the folder, so I could not apply it. The checkbox fixes could change a $0 result, and a fixed cite could change which qualifier a row has.
- The Grep check treats any $ after the cite as a headline amount. It does not check that a $ is followed by digits, so a bare "$" would be miscounted. I did not test for this.
```

## H4-r2

arm `folder`, model `claude-haiku-5-5`, tools `Read,Grep,Glob`, effort `low`, wall 21.1 s

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Qualifier outcomes: a case has qualifier "expire", "prior" or "out" if its cite ends with that word in parentheses, on any base section. Among cases with each of those three qualifiers separately, what percentage have a headline penalty of $0? Round each to one decimal.
```

**Expire: 35.7%** (185 of 518 cases). **Prior: 21.7%** (59 of 272). **Out: 18.3%** (35 of 191).

I could not run the helper script because Bash is disabled in this session. These figures come from Grep counts on `reference/cases.csv`, not from `cases.py`, so treat them as a hand-check of the tool's result.

- **Method:** a case counts as qualifier X if its cite contains `(X)`. A case counts as headline $0 if its line has no `$` anywhere. This matches the definition, since no `$` means headline $0.
- **Totals:** `expire` 518, `prior` 272 and `out` 191 cases. The qualifier counts come from the cite field, and the zero counts are the no-`$` lines among them.
- **Row check:** the file has 9,959 lines, which is the header plus 9,958 rows, so no quoted field breaks a row across lines.
- **Caveats:**
  - A `$` sign followed by no digit (e.g. "$ TBD") would have headline $0 by the definition, but my no-`$` test would miss it. The one check I ran only surfaced `$$1000` rows, which have a headline of $1,000, so I found no such case in the first 20 matches. I did not check the rest.
  - The match is on whole lines, so a qualifier string appearing in another column would be counted. I saw no sign of that.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Answers (Bash was disabled in this session, so I could not run `cases.py`. I computed these with Grep counts on `reference/cases.csv`):**

- **expire:** 185 of 518 cases have a headline penalty of $0 → **35.7%**
- **prior:** 59 of 272 cases have a headline penalty of $0 → **21.7%**
- **out:** 35 of 191 cases have a headline penalty of $0 → **18.3%**

**Reading taken:** "Cases" means rows, one recommendation form each, as the definitions say. The zero-penalty count is the cases whose recommendation has no dollar amount. Per the definition, "Training or $5,000 if…" has headline $5,000, so it is not counted as zero.

**Method:**
- Qualifier totals (518, 272, 191) match the counts in `SCHEMA.md`.
- Cases with a dollar sign after the cite: expire 333, prior 213, out 156. Every dollar sign after the cite is followed by a digit, so these are all nonzero headlines.
- Zero = total − nonzero: expire 518 − 333 = 185; prior 272 − 213 = 59; out 191 − 156 = 35.
- I checked that no "$" sits in the additional-info column for these rows. The count is line-based, so it assumes one CSV line per row. The qualifier totals matching `SCHEMA.md` supports that.

**Receipts (zero-penalty examples):**
- expire: `ord_PSD_011525.pdf` page 26, case 32683, "Warning letter" (in the Warning letter wording); the list of expire lines with no "$" is in the Grep output.
- prior: `ord_PSD_032122.pdf` page 8, case 24489, "Warning letter".
- out: `ord_PSD_012622.pdf` page 158, case 24121, "Warning Letter Special instructions: dig tickets".

To check with the tool, run from the folder root: `python3 reference/tools/cases.py count --qual expire --zero-penalty`, and the same with `prior` and `out`. Expected output is 185, 59 and 35 of 518, 272 and 191.
```

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

2023 to 2024 shift: (a) among non-utility respondent cases, what percentage have a headline penalty of at least $5,000 in 2023 and in 2024; and (b) what percentage of all cases are utility respondent cases in 2023 and in 2024? Denominators include $0 cases. Round each percentage to one decimal.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**(a) Non-utility respondents with headline penalty ≥ $5,000 (by order year):**
- **2023: 41.5%** (623 of 1,502 non-utility cases)
- **2024: 48.3%** (687 of 1,421 non-utility cases)

**(b) Utility-respondent share of all cases (by order year):**
- **2023: 28.7%** (605 of 2,107 cases)
- **2024: 25.1%** (476 of 1,897 cases)

**Caveat: these counts come from regex searches, not from the `cases.py` tool.** Bash was disabled in this session, so I could not run the tool. I counted with Grep on `reference/cases.csv`, using these patterns:
- Total cases per year: lines starting `ord_…,2023-` or `ord_…,2024-`.
- Utility: respondent starts with one of the 13 PREAMBLE prefixes, ignoring case and leading spaces and quotes.
- Headline ≥ $5,000: the first `$` amount after the cite is 5,000 or more. Thousands commas and a space after a comma are allowed, and a doubled `$` is handled. Amounts without a `$` count as $0.
- Non-utility ≥ $5,000 = all ≥ $5,000 minus utility ≥ $5,000. Those counts were 638 − 15 = 623 for 2023 and 699 − 12 = 687 for 2024.

Other caveats:
- I did not produce receipts, because the regex approach cannot list example rows with the right filters.

  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.

  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**(a) Non-utility cases with headline penalty ≥ $5,000**
- 2023: **623 of 1,502 = 41.5%**
- 2024: **687 of 1,421 = 48.3%**

**(b) Utility-respondent share of all cases**
- 2023: **605 of 2,107 = 28.7%**
- 2024: **476 of 1,897 = 25.1%**

Non-utility counts are total minus utility, so 2,107 − 605 = 1,502 and 1,897 − 476 = 1,421.

**Caveats**
- **The helper tool did not run.** Bash was unavailable, so I could not run `cases.py`. I got the same counts by running Grep regexes on `reference/cases.csv`, using the PREAMBLE definitions: year from the order date, the utility prefix list, and the first `$` amount in the recommendation, with a doubled `$` and a space after a comma handled. Running `python3 reference/tools/cases.py count --year 2023 --utility no --penalty-min 5000` (and the 2024 and utility variants) should confirm these figures.
- **Checks on the regexes.** The total row count is 9,958, as expected. Every 2023–2024 row with a `$` sign has it inside the recommendation field, so no amounts were picked up from other columns. The `$$1000` rows and the `$3, 500` row fall below $5,000 and are classed correctly.
- **Row-level receipts were not produced.** Listing example rows would need the tool or a full read of the file.
- **Known parse defects remain.** The SCHEMA lists 16 OCR cite errors and some scan-page field errors. These affect cite and name fields, which these two ratios do not use.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Answer:** (a) Non-utility cases with headline penalty ≥ $5,000: **41.4%** in 2023 (622 of 1,502) and **48.3%** in 2024 (687 of 1,421). (b) Utility-respondent share of all cases: **28.7%** in 2023 (605 of 2,107) and **25.1%** in 2024 (476 of 1,897).

**Reading taken:** Year is the order-date year. Headline penalty is the first "$" amount in the recommendation, with the doubled "$" and space-after-comma rules applied. Denominators include $0 cases. Utility respondent uses the 13-name prefix test, ignoring case and leading spaces.

**Method and limits:** I could not run `cases.py`, because I have no shell tool here. Instead I counted rows in `reference/cases.csv` with regex searches anchored on the order date, respondent and first "$" amount. The raw counts were:

| Count | 2023 | 2024 |
|---|---|---|
| All cases | 2,107 | 1,897 |
| Utility respondent | 605 | 476 |
| Non-utility respondent | 1,502 | 1,421 |
| Headline ≥ $5,000, all | 637 | 699 |
| Headline ≥ $5,000, utility | 15 | 12 |
| Headline ≥ $5,000, non-utility | 622 | 687 |

**Receipts:** I did not pull example rows, because the regex counts do not list rows.

**Caveats:**
- The regex assumes "$" appears only in the recommendation field before the abstentions and notes. I did not check this against every row.
- To confirm, run `python3 reference/tools/cases.py count --year 2023 --utility no --penalty-min 5000 --receipts 3` (and the same for 2024). Compare with the non-utility totals above.
```
